A Classical Linear λ \lambda -Calculus based on Contraposition
Pablo Barenbaum, Eduardo Bonelli, Leopoldo Lerena

Abstract
We present a novel linear λ \lambda -calculus for Classical Multiplicative Exponential Linear Logic ( MELL ) along the lines of the propositions-as-types paradigm. Starting from the standard term assignment for Intuitionistic Multiplicative Linear Logic ( IMLL ), we observe that if we incorporate linear negation, its involutive nature implies that both A ⊸ B A\multimap B and B ⟂ ⊸ A ⟂ B^{\perp}\multimap A^{\perp} should have the same proofs. The introduction of a linear modus tollens rule, stating that from B ⟂ ⊸ A ⟂ B^{\perp}\multimap A^{\perp} and A A we may conclude B B , allows one to recover classical MLL . Furthermore, a term assignment for this elimination rule,the study of proof normalization in a λ \lambda -calculus with this elimination rule prompts us to define the novel notion of contra-substitution t { a \ \ s } t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}} . Introduced alongside linear substitution, contra-substitution denotes the term that results from “grabbing” the unique occurrence of a a in t t and “pulling” from it, in order to turn the term t t inside out (much like a sock) and then replacing a a with s s . We call the one-sided natural deduction presentation of classical MLL , the λ MLL \lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}} -calculus. Guided by the behavior of contra-substitution in the presence of the exponentials, we extend it to a similar presentation for MELL . We prove that this calculus is sound and complete with respect to MELL and that it satisfies the standard properties of a typed programming language: subject reduction, confluence and strong normalization. Moreover, we show that several well-known term assignments for classical logic can be encoded in λ MELL \lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}} . These include Parigot’s λ ​ μ \lambda\mu [ 25 ] both via Danos, Joinet and Schellinx’s T and Q-Translations [ 14 ] , Curien and Herbelin’s λ ¯ ​ μ ​ μ ~ \overline{\lambda}\mu\tilde{\mu} -calculus [ 13 ] , and Hasegawa’s μ ​ DCLL \mu\textsf{DCLL} [ 20 , 19 ] .

1
Introduction

Linear Logic (
LL
)
[
18
]

proposes a
resource conscious
approach to logic,
in which formulae cannot be arbitrarily duplicated or erased.
It incorporates two modalities, called the
exponential modalities
,
that recover the ability to duplicate and erase formulae in a controlled way.
While the
of-course
modality
{!}{A}

represents the
obligation
to duplicate and erase the formula
A

as many times as required,
the
why-not
modality
{?}A

represents the
right
to duplicate and erase the formula
A

as many times as desired.
This makes
LL
a suitable language to model resource-sensitive phenomena
such as concurrency, memory management, and computational complexity.
To explore the computational consequences of these ideas, one can turn to
the
proposition-as-types paradigm
, which allows to interpret
propositions as types, proofs as programs,
and proof normalization as program evaluation.
The canonical example
is the correspondence between minimal logic
and simply typed
\lambda
-calculus.
The proposition-as-types paradigm has been extended to many other logical systems,
and in particular to Linear Logic and various of its fragments.

Tensions Between Classical Symmetries and Lambda Calculi.

Linear Logic is known to exhibit numerous symmetries.
This is perhaps most evident in
sequent calculus
presentations, in which the left rules for a logical connective (
e.g.
“
{!}{}
”)
are the mirror image of the right rules for the dual connective
(
e.g.
“
{?}
”).
These kinds of symmetries are also present in Classical Logic (
CL
),
but notably not in Intuitionistic Logic.
For instance, in
CL
and
LL
,
affirming a proposition
A
as a thesis corresponds
exactly to denying
A
as a hypothesis—the
key behind the classical principle of
reductio ad absurdum
.
Another related symmetry is expressed in the classical law of
contraposition
,
which states that
A\to B
is equivalent to
\neg{B}\to\neg{A}
.
One essential aspect of this symmetry is that one would expect to be able
to define negation as an involutive connective, so that
\neg\neg A=A

becomes a strict equality, which in particular means that a
cut
between
a proof of
A
and one of its negation
\neg{A}
does not prioritize
either side. Compare this with the intuitionistic case, in which

\neg{A}
corresponds to a function type
A\to\bot
, but an arbitrary
type
B
cannot be understood as a function type in general.

When a logic is presented in (single-conclusion)
natural deduction
style,
the computational formalism one obtains is a variant of the

\lambda
-calculus.
It is not obvious how to formulate systems in
this style that exhibit classical symmetries
and still enjoy good computational properties such as canonicity.
For example, in the case of classical propositional logic,
Barbanera and Berardi’s classical
\lambda
-calculus
[
7
]

is symmetric but non-confluent,
while Parigot’s
\lambda\mu
-calculus
[
25
]

is confluent but non-symmetric (
e.g.
negation is not involutive
1
1

1

Not only is
\neg\neg A\neq A
, but
\neg\neg A\supset A
is not provable
[
3
, Cor.1]
.
).
In the case of
LL
, it is not clear how to formulate appropriate
natural deduction rules for logical connectives
that involve sequents with more than one formula on the right.
For example, the right introduction rule for multiplicative conjunction derives

\Gamma\vdash A\invamp B
from
\Gamma\vdash A,B
,
and the right contraction rule for the why-not modality derives

\Gamma\vdash{?}A
from
\Gamma\vdash{?}A,{?}A
.

Intuitionistic and Sequent-Based Systems.

The difficulty to harmonize classical symmetries and lambda calculi
is perhaps the reason why most of the existing literature that studies

LL
from the point of view of the propositions-as-types paradigm
takes one of two routes.
On one hand, some systems such as
ILL
or
DILL

[
8
,
9
]

restrict
LL
to the
intuitionistic
fragment.
Intuitionistic formulations of
LL
sidestep the aforementioned difficulties
by restricting the possible “shapes” of formulae and sequents,
in such a way that a judgement involves a number of
input
formulae and a
single distinguished
output
formula.
For instance, in
ILL
, the multiplicative disjunction is removed in favor of
the (more restricted) linear implication, and the why-not modality
is removed altogether.
These restrictions allow to formulate well-behaved
intuitionistic linear lambda calculi, in which
the proof of a sequent
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}\vdash B

is understood as a single sequential program taking
n
inputs and
producing one output.
However, this is at the expense of losing the classical symmetries,
and ruling out a large class of formulae and proofs,
leaving the underlying computational mechanisms unexplored.

On the other hand, there are systems corresponding to unrestricted
(
i.e.
classical)
LL
, which are usually derived from
sequent calculus

presentations of
LL
.
As already mentioned, sequent-based presentations are symmetric,
and cut elimination, in this setting, exhibits good properties such as strong
normalization and confluence.
Formulae do not play a distinguished input or output role: the proof of a sequent
\vdash A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}
is understood
as the parallel composition of
n
interacting processes
[
1
,
28
,
29
]
. Calculi based on these principles can thus be seen
as concurrent systems, more akin to process calculi than to
the
\lambda
-calculus.
Among these systems, one also finds graphical formalisms like
proof-nets
.
They are intentionally designed to abstract away the permutative rules
of sequent calculus. This allows reasoning at a higher level,
but at the same time it makes it difficult to reason axiomatically: for
example, to formalize proof-nets in a proof
assistant one needs to choose concrete terms as representatives of proof-nets,
and the need for explicit permutation rules reappears.

This leaves open the question of whether a single-conclusion
natural deduction system for (classical)
LL
can be designed that
retains the classical symmetries and desirable computational properties.
Such a system, in which proofs can still be understood as “functional”
programs, could form the basis of linear functional programming languages
and proof assistants.

Towards a Classical Linear
\lambda
-Calculus.

Our starting point is a natural deduction system
with
multiplicative conjunction
(
\otimes
)
and
linear implication
(
\multimap
) as the only connectives,

i.e.
corresponding to
Multiplicative Linear Logic
(
MLL
).
An involutive negation operator
(\cdot)^{\perp}
can be defined essentially by the equation

(A\multimap B)^{\perp}=A\otimes B^{\perp}
,
and its dual.
The elimination rule for linear implication in the
intuitionistic

fragment of
MLL
is the linear
modus ponens
rule, which states that
from
A\multimap B
and
A
one may conclude
B
.
A first observation is that the only missing piece to recover

classical

MLL
is to add the linear
modus tollens
rule,
which states that
from
A\multimap B
and
B^{\perp}
one may conclude
A^{\perp}
.
This results in an inference system that
—from the strictly logical point of view—
turns out to prove all and only the valid sequents of
MLL
.
Also, it raises the question of how to provide a computational interpretation
for it, that is, to devise a proof normalization procedure.
The key to define the computational interpretation are the standard operation
of
substitution
and, to the best of our knowledge, a new operation
we call
contra-substitution
.

The Linear Contra-Substitution Principle.

Modus ponens
corresponds to

application
: a linear function
t:A\multimap B

may be applied to an argument
s:A

to yield a result
t\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,s:B
.
From the computational point of view,
a
redex
formed by the interaction between a
\lambda
-abstraction
and an application can be normalized by means of the usual

\beta
-rule, performing the simplification

(\lambda a.\,t)\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,s\rightsquigarrow t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}
.
The right-hand side,
t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}
, denotes the
linear substitution

of the (unique) free occurrence of
a
in
t
by
s
.
The
modus tollens
rule corresponds to a comparatively less familiar
operation we dub
contra-application
:
a linear function
t:A\multimap B

may be “contra-applied” to a term
s:B^{\perp}

to yield a result
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}s:A^{\perp}
.
A
redex
formed by the interaction between a
\lambda
-abstraction
and a contra-application can be normalized by means of a
new rule that performs the simplification

(\lambda a.\,t){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}s\rightsquigarrow t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}
.
The right hand side,
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}
corresponds to a non-standard
operation we dub
contra-substitution
.
Intuitively,
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}
is the expression that results
from “grabbing” the
unique
occurrence of
a
in
t
and “pulling”
from it, in order to turn the term
t
inside out.
This operation is defined by structural induction on
t
. For example,
if
\langle t,a\rangle:A\otimes B
is a tensor pair,
and
s:A\multimap B^{\perp}
,
then
\langle t,a\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}

turns out to produce the term
s\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,t:B^{\perp}
where
s

is applied to
t
.
Uniqueness of the occurrence of
a
(
i.e.
linearity)
is crucial to be able to define this notion.

Summary of contributions.

We explore a propositions-as-types correspondence for a one-sided natural deduction presentation of
Multiplicative Exponential Linear Logic
(
MELL
) based on the novel notion of
contra-substitution
, where proofs are modeled as functional expressions rather than processes. The resulting
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
-calculus provides term witnesses for all proofs in
MELL
, is strongly normalizing and confluent. It makes use of a notion of structural equivalence that is a strong bisimulation and we believe has an interest of its own. Several well-known term assignments for classical logic are shown to be simulated in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
including Parigot’s
\lambda\mu

[
25
]
, Hasegawa’s
\mu\textsf{DCLL}

[
20
,
19
]
(in the appendix), and Curien and Herbelin’s
\overline{\lambda}\mu\tilde{\mu}
-calculus
[
13
]
. Some proof skeletons and additional definitions are available in the appendix. See
[
10
]
for full proofs.

2
Preliminaries

This section recalls
MLL
and
MELL
(with units) via the usual one-sided sequent presentation.

Multiplicative Linear Logic.

We assume given a denumerable set
of
atomic formulae

\alpha,\beta,\ldots

each with its corresponding negative version
\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta},\ldots
.
The set of
MLL
-formulae
is given by:

A,B,\ldots::=\alpha\mid\overline{\alpha}\mid A\otimes B\mid A\multimap B

The logical connectives
\otimes
and
\multimap
are called
multiplicative
.
Linear negation is the involutive operator
(\cdot)^{\perp}
defined by:

\alpha^{\perp}:=\overline{\alpha}\quad\quad\quad\overline{\alpha}^{\perp}:=\alpha\quad\quad\quad(A\otimes B)^{\perp}:=A\multimap B^{\perp}\quad\quad\quad(A\multimap B)^{\perp}:=A\otimes B^{\perp}

In
MLL
, we take linear implication (
\multimap
) as a primitive connective
rather than multiplicative disjunction (
\invamp
), which is the more conventional
choice.
This is just a minor presentational point, since
\multimap
and
\invamp

are interdefinable; indeed, one can define
A\invamp B:=A^{\perp}\multimap B
.

Judgements in
MLL
are of the form
\vdash{\Gamma}

where
\Gamma
is a finite
multiset
of
MLL
formulae.
Note that working with multisets avoids the need of an explicit exchange rule.
We write
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}
for the multiset union of finite multisets
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Valid
MLL
judgement are defined inductively by the following rules.

Definition 1

(Valid
MLL
judgement)
.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 13.72217pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{{}^{@}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=34.08325pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-ax}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{A,A^{\perp}}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash{\Gamma,A}\quad\vdash{\Gamma^{\prime},A^{\perp}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=69.9444pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-cut}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 20.65279pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash{\Gamma,A}\quad\vdash{\Gamma^{\prime},B}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=64.78119pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-$\otimes$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 1.9445pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},A\otimes B}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 3.23608pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash{\Gamma,A^{\perp},B}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=51.83665pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-$\multimap$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{\Gamma,A\multimap B}$}}}}\end{array}

Multiplicative Exponential Linear Logic.

The set of
MELL
-formulae
is given by:

A,B,\ldots::=\alpha\mid\overline{\alpha}\mid A\otimes B\mid A\invamp B\mid\text{1}\mid{\perp}\mid{!}{A}\mid{?}A

The connectives
\otimes
and
\invamp
,
as well as the units
1
and
{\perp}
are called
multiplicative
,
while the modalities
{!}{}
and
{?}
are called
exponential
.
Linear negation is the involutive operator
(\cdot)^{\perp}
defined by:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l@{\HS\HS}r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l@{\HS\HS}r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l@{\HS\HS}r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l}\alpha^{\perp}&:=&\overline{\alpha}\hfil\quad\quad&\overline{\alpha}^{\perp}&:=&\alpha\hfil\quad\quad&(A\otimes B)^{\perp}&:=&A^{\perp}\invamp B^{\perp}\hfil\quad\quad&(A\invamp B)^{\perp}&:=&A^{\perp}\otimes B^{\perp}\\
({!}{A})^{\perp}&:=&{?}A^{\perp}\hfil\quad\quad&({?}A)^{\perp}&:=&{!}{A^{\perp}}\hfil\quad\quad&\text{1}^{\perp}&:=&{\perp}\hfil\quad\quad&{\perp}^{\perp}&:=&\text{1}\end{array}

Unlike in
MLL
, we take multiplicative disjunction (
\invamp
) as the primitive
connective in
MELL
. Again, this is just a minor point to improve presentation,
and in
MELL
one can define
A\multimap B:=A^{\perp}\invamp B
.
Judgements are of the form
\vdash{\Gamma}

where
\Gamma
is a finite multiset of
MELL
formulae.
Valid
MELL
judgement are defined inductively by the following rules.

Definition 2

(Valid
MELL
judgement)
.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 13.72217pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{{}^{@}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=34.08325pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-ax}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{A,A^{\perp}}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash{\Gamma,A}\quad\vdash{\Gamma^{\prime},A^{\perp}}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=69.9444pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-cut}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 20.65279pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash{\Gamma,A}\quad\vdash{\Gamma^{\prime},B}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=64.78119pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-$\otimes$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 1.9445pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},A\otimes B}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 3.74997pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash{\Gamma,A,B}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=47.11443pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-$\invamp$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{\Gamma,A\invamp B}$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 3.62495pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{{}^{@}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=13.88882pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-$\text{1}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{\text{1}}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 5.1389pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash{\Gamma}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=25.41664pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-${\perp}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{\Gamma,{\perp}}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.55559pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash{{?}\Gamma,A}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=32.91667pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-!p}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{{?}\Gamma,{!}{A}}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 7.50002pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash{\Gamma}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=30.13887pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-?w}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{\Gamma,{?}A}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.5278pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash{\Gamma,A}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=30.13887pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-?d}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{\Gamma,{?}A}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash{\Gamma,{?}A,{?}A}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=45.1389pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{l-?c}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 7.50002pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash{\Gamma,{?}A}$}}}}\end{array}

3
A Contraposition-Based Calculus for
MLL

This section presents the
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
-calculus
, our propositions-as-types interpretation of
MLL
.
The set of
terms
and
case contexts
are given by:

\begin{array}[]{llrll}(\text{Terms})&t,s,\ldots&::=&a\,\mid\,\langle t,s\rangle\,\mid\,{s}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\,\mid\,\lambda a.\,t\,\mid\,t\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,s\,\mid\,t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}s\\
(\text{Case contexts})&\mathsf{L}&::=&\Box\,\mid\,{\mathsf{L}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\end{array}

Terms include standard constructs of linear
\lambda
-calculus: linear variables
a,b,\ldots
, ranging over a countably infinite set; tensor introduction
\langle t,s\rangle
and elimination
{t}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}
; lambda abstraction
\lambda a.\,t
and application
t\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,s
. Moreover, they include a novel constructor: the
contra-application

t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}s
.
From the logical point of view, application and contra-application both correspond
to eliminations of the implication.
As mentioned before, while application corresponds to modus ponens, contra-application corresponds to modus tollens.
Free and bound occurrences of variables are defined as expected,
where
{t}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}

binds free occurrences of
a,b
in
t
,
and
\lambda a.\,t
binds free occurrences of
a
in
t
.
Terms are considered up to
\alpha
-renaming of bound variables.

Case contexts are sequences of tensor eliminations with a subterm hole (
\Box
) that can be filled by any given
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
term.
We write
{\mathsf{L}}\bm{\langle}{t}\bm{\rangle}
(or also
t\mathsf{L}
) for the term resulting from replacing the unique occurrence of the hole in
\mathsf{L}
with
t
,
possibly capturing free variables in
t
in the process.
We shall use case contexts for reduction at a distance.

Typing judgements are of the form
\Gamma\vdash t:A

where
A
is a
MLL
formula
and
\Gamma
is a
typing environment
,
a partial function mapping variables to
MLL
formulae,
written
\Gamma=(a_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,a_{n}:A_{n})

and assumed to be of finite domain.
Valid judgements are given by:

Definition 3

(Valid
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
typing judgements)
.

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 23.63292pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{{}^{@}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=53.90475pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{m-ax}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle a:A\vdash a:A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A\quad\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash s:B$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=86.41281pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{m-i$\otimes$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 3.3332pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\langle t,s\rangle:A\otimes B$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A\otimes B\quad\Gamma^{\prime},a:A,b:B\vdash s:C$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=159.99475pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{m-e$\otimes$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 35.44757pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash{s}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}:C$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 7.08336pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma,a:A\vdash t:B$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=77.67792pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{m-i$\multimap$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda a.\,t:A\multimap B$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A\multimap B\quad\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash s:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=110.57939pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{m-e$\multimap_{1}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 23.33325pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,s:B$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash t:A\multimap B\quad\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash s:B^{\perp}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=116.91618pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{m-e$\multimap_{2}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 25.58664pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}s:A^{\perp}$}}}}\end{array}

Rules are presented in introduction/elimination pairs style of natural deduction, where the most noteworthy point is that linear implication admits two different elimination rules.
The first,
m-e
\multimap_{1}
(modus ponens) corresponds to the standard application of terms from
\lambda
-calculus.
The second,
m-e
\multimap_{2}
(modus tollens) is the typing rule of our new construct, contra-application.
This latter is the only strictly classical rule in our typing system.
The following result justifies that the
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
calculus corresponds
to
MLL
from the point of view of propositions-as-types.
The statement of the lemma relies on an abuse of notation:
if
\Gamma
is a multiset of formulae
\Gamma=(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n})
,
we write
\Gamma^{\perp}\vdash t:B

to mean
a_{1}:A_{1}^{\perp},\ldots,a_{n}:A_{n}^{\perp}\vdash t:B

where
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
is a set of distinct linear variables.

Lemma 4

(name=Soundness and Completeness of
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}

[Proof on pg.
A
]
, restate=[name=Completeness and Soundness of
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
]CompletenessSoundnessMLL)
.

•

Soundness.

If
\Gamma\vdash t:A
holds in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
then
\vdash{\Gamma^{\perp},A}
holds in
MLL
.

•

Completeness.

If
\vdash{\Gamma_{0}}
holds in
MLL
and
\Gamma,A
is any permutation of
\Gamma_{0}
,
there exists a term
t
such that

\Gamma^{\perp}\vdash t:A

holds in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
.

Note that if a sequent
\vdash{A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}}
holds in
MLL
,
completeness allows to select
any
of the
A_{i}
as the thesis,
leaving the negation of the remaining formulae
(
A_{1}^{\perp},\ldots,A_{i-1}^{\perp},A_{i+1}^{\perp},\ldots,A_{n}^{\perp}
)
as the hypotheses.
For example, the valid
MLL
sequent
\vdash{A^{\perp},B^{\perp},A\otimes B}

has three possible “readings” in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
, all of them valid.
Completeness simultaneously ensures the existence of three terms
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
,
t_{3}
such that

a:B,b:A\multimap B^{\perp}\vdash t_{1}:A^{\perp}

and
a:A,b:A\multimap B^{\perp}\vdash t_{2}:B^{\perp}

and
a:A,b:B\vdash t_{3}:A\otimes B
.
Moreover, the proof of completeness is constructive, so
from the derivation in
MLL
the terms
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
, and
t_{3}

can be effectively recovered.

Remark 5
.

Completeness fails if
m-e
\multimap_{2}
is absent.
For example, is easy to see that the sequent

\vdash{A\otimes(C\multimap C),A^{\perp}\otimes(B\multimap B)}

is valid in
MLL
.
However, there is no term
t
and variable
a
such that

a:A\multimap(C\otimes C^{\perp})\vdash t:A^{\perp}\otimes(B\multimap B)
or

a:A^{\perp}\multimap(B\otimes B)\vdash t:A\otimes(C\multimap C)
are derivable in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
if
m-e
\multimap_{2}
is absent.
Both are derivable in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
. For example:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\begin{array}[]{c}\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty a:A\multimap(C\otimes C^{\perp})\vdash a:A\multimap(C\otimes C^{\perp})\quad\cdot\vdash\lambda c.\,c:C\multimap C$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=251.51198pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{m-e$\multimap_{2}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 49.8914pt\hbox{$\displaystyle a:A\multimap(C\otimes C^{\perp})\vdash a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}(\lambda c.\,c):A^{\perp}$}}}}\end{array}\quad\cdot\vdash\lambda b.\,b:B\multimap B$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=379.92148pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\textsc{{m-i$\otimes$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 76.02325pt\hbox{$\displaystyle a:A\multimap(C\otimes C^{\perp})\vdash\langle a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}(\lambda c.\,c),\lambda b.\,b\rangle:A^{\perp}\otimes(B\multimap B)$}}}}\end{array}

Linear terms.

A term is
linear
if each free variable occurs exactly once, and there is exactly one occurrence of each bound variable inside the scope of its binder.
Typable terms are linear:
If
\Gamma\vdash t:B
, then
t
is linear
and the variables in
\Gamma
are exactly the free variables of
t
.

Substitution and contra-substitution.

Reduction semantics for the
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
-calculus relies on two substitution operations.
First,
linear substitution

t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}
denotes the standard capture-avoiding substitution of a linear variable
a
by a term
s
in
t
.
This operation is compatible with typing in the sense that if
\Gamma,a:A\vdash t:B
and
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash s:A
hold in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
, then so does
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}:B
.
Second, we introduce
contra-substitution
, written
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}
.

Definition 6

(Contra-substitution for
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
)
.

Let
t
be a linear term and let
a
be a free linear variable
such that
a\in\mathsf{fv}(t)
.
Let us write “
\bm{\ast}_{i}
” to abbreviate the condition
a\in\mathsf{fv}(t_{i})
.
The contra-substitution operation
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}

is defined by induction on the structure of
t
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l@{\HS}r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l}\lx@intercol a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}:=s\hskip 28.45274pt(t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle b,c\rangle:=t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}:=\begin{cases}t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle b,c\rangle:=t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\text{if $\bm{\ast}_{1}$, $a\notin\{b,c\}$}\\
t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\lambda b.\,t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}c\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&\text{if $\bm{\ast}_{2}$}\end{cases}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
(\lambda b.\,t^{\prime}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&{t^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash c{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle b,c\rangle:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\hfil\quad&\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&\begin{cases}t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&\text{if $\bm{\ast}_{1}$}\\
t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&\text{if $\bm{\ast}_{2}$}\end{cases}\\
(t_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,t_{2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&\begin{cases}t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\langle t_{2},s\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&\text{if $\bm{\ast}_{1}$}\\
t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&\text{if $\bm{\ast}_{2}$}\end{cases}\hfil\quad&(t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}t_{2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&\begin{cases}t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\langle s,t_{2}\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&\text{if $\bm{\ast}_{1}$}\\
t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash t_{1}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&\text{if $\bm{\ast}_{2}$}\end{cases}\end{array}

For example,
(\lambda b.\,a\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,b){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}=(a\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,b){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash c{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle b,c\rangle:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}=a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\langle b,c\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle b,c\rangle:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}=\langle b,c\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle b,c\rangle:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}
.
Contra-substitution too is compatible with typing:

Lemma 7

(name=Type Compatibility of Contra-substitution for MLL
[Proof on pg.
A
]
, restate=[name=Type Compatibility of Contrasubstitution for MLL]ContrasubstitutionLemmaMLL)
.

If
\Gamma,a:A\vdash t:B
and
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash s:B^{\perp}
hold in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
, then so does
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}:A^{\perp}
.

Reduction in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
.

The reduction relation
\to
in the
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
-calculus is defined over

linear terms
, as the contextual closure of the three following reduction rules:

\begin{array}[]{llll}(\lambda a.\,t)\mathsf{L}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,s&\mapsto_{\mathsf{{\beta}{\lambda}{L}}}&t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\mathsf{L}\\
(\lambda a.\,t)\mathsf{L}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}s&\mapsto_{\mathsf{{\beta}{\lambda}{R}}}&t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}\mathsf{L}\\
{t}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=\langle s,r\rangle\mathsf{L}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\mapsto_{\mathsf{{\beta}{\otimes}}}&t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\mathsf{L}&\text{($b\notin\mathsf{fv}(s)$)}\end{array}

Note that the restriction over linear terms is necessary
for the right-hand side of the rule
\mapsto_{\mathsf{{\beta}{\lambda}{R}}}
to be well-defined, as contra-substitution is
not defined for arbitrary terms.
Due to the presence of the case contexts, these rules are said to
operate “at a distance”. For example,

(\lambda a.\,t){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle b,c\rangle:=r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,s
is a redex,
even though the
\lambda
-abstraction and the application are not directly
in “contact” with each other.

Strong normalization and confluence.

We define the
size
of a
linear
term
t
, denoted
\mathsf{sz}(t)
, as the number of term constructors in
t
.
Assuming that
a\in\mathsf{fv}(t)
, this measure satisfies the following two properties:

\mathsf{sz}(t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}})=\mathsf{sz}(t)+\mathsf{sz}(s)
and
\mathsf{sz}(t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}})=\mathsf{sz}(t)+\mathsf{sz}(s)
,
due to linearity.
It is then easy to see that every reduction step strictly decreases the size of the term;
hence the
\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
-calculus is strongly normalizing.
By Newman’s Lemma, confluence is then reduced to local confluence (
i.e.
the weak Church–Rosser property),
which can be proved through a systematic case analysis of all coinitial steps.

Proposition 8
.

\lambda_{\textsf{{MLL}}}
is strongly normalizing and confluent.

4
A Contraposition-Based Calculus for
MELL

This section presents
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
, our propositions-as-types calculus based on contra-substitution for
MELL
.
The set of
terms
and
positive elimination contexts
are given by:

\begin{array}[]{llrll}(\text{Terms})&t,s,\ldots&::=&a\,|\,u\,|\,\langle t,s\rangle\,|\,t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\,|\,{\invamp}(a,b).t\,|\,t\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,s\,|\,t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}s\\
&&\mid&{!}{a.t}\,|\,t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{u}:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\,|\,{?}u.t\,|\,{t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{a}s}\,|\,t\lightning s\,|\,\star\,|\,t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\\
(\text{Positive elim. contexts})&\mathsf{L}&::=&\Box\,|\,\mathsf{L}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\,|\,\mathsf{L}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{u}:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\,|\,\mathsf{L}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\end{array}

Terms include: linear variables
a,b,\ldots
ranging over a countably infinite set; unrestricted variables
u,v,\ldots
ranging over a countably infinite set; tensor introduction
\langle t,s\rangle
and elimination
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}
; par introduction
{\invamp}(a,b).t
; application
t\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,s
; contra-application
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}s
; of-course introduction
{!}{a.t}
and elimination
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{u}:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}
; why-not introduction
{?}u.t
and elimination
{t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{a}s}
; contradiction
t\lightning s
; unit introduction
\star
and elimination
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}
. Free and bound occurrences of variables are defined as expected,
where
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}
and
{\invamp}(a,b).t

binds free occurrences of
a,b
in
t
;

{!}{a.t}
and
{t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{a}s}
bind free occurrences of
a
in
t
;
and
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{u}:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}
and
{?}u.t
bind free occurrences of
u
in
t
. Terms are considered up to
\alpha
-renaming of bound variables.
We call expressions of the form

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{u}:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}

positive eliminators
2
2

2

This terminology stems from polarized linear logic.
.
Positive eliminators are collectively denoted by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\mathtt{p}:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}
, where
\mathtt{p}
is called a
pattern
,
and

\mathsf{fv}(\mathtt{p})
is defined as:
\mathsf{fv}(\langle a,b\rangle)=\{a,b\}
,
\mathsf{fv}({!}{u})=\{u\}
,
and
\mathsf{fv}(\star)=\varnothing
.

Judgements
have the form
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash t:A
, where
A
is a
MELL
formula,
\Delta
(resp.
\Gamma
) is an
unrestricted (resp. linear) typing environment
,

i.e.
a partial function mapping unrestricted (resp. linear) variables to types.
Derivable judgements are defined in Fig.
1
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 30.0218pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{{}^{@}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=66.68253pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-ax}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;a:A\vdash a:A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 31.84943pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{{}^{@}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=70.33777pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-uax}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta,u:A;\cdot\vdash u:A$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Delta;\Gamma\vdash t:A\quad\Delta;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash s:B$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=111.96835pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-i$\otimes$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 9.72209pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\langle t,s\rangle:A\otimes B$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Delta;\Gamma\vdash s:A\otimes B\quad\Delta;\Gamma^{\prime},a:A,b:B\vdash t:C$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=185.5503pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-e$\otimes$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 41.83646pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash{t}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}:C$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 6.16678pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Delta;\Gamma,a:A,b:B\vdash t:{\perp}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=110.4141pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-i$\invamp$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;\Gamma\vdash{\invamp}(a,b).t:A^{\perp}\invamp B^{\perp}$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Delta;\Gamma\vdash t:A\invamp B\quad\Delta;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash s:A^{\perp}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=137.1627pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-e$\invamp_{1}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 30.23602pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,s:B$}}}}\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Delta;\Gamma\vdash t:A\invamp B\quad\Delta;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash s:B^{\perp}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=137.7495pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-e$\invamp_{2}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 32.48941pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}s:A$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Delta;a:A\vdash t:{\perp}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=62.23003pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-i${!}{}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 3.65259pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;\cdot\vdash{!}{a.t}:{!}{A^{\perp}}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Delta;\Gamma\vdash s:{!}{A}\quad\Delta,u:A;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:B$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=137.97064pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-e${!}{}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 23.1943pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{u}:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}:B$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Delta,u:A^{\perp};\Gamma\vdash t:{\perp}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=79.11314pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-i${?}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 7.45822pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;\Gamma\vdash{?}u.t:{?}A$}}}}\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Delta;\Gamma\vdash s:{?}A\quad\Delta;a:A\vdash t:{\perp}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=125.3896pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-e${?}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 31.75171pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;\Gamma\vdash{t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{a}s}:{\perp}$}}}}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Delta;\Gamma\vdash t:B\quad\Delta;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash s:B^{\perp}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=118.30515pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-i${\perp}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 21.23952pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t\lightning s:{\perp}$}}}}\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 13.06947pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vphantom{{}^{@}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=32.77786pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-i$\text{1}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;\cdot\vdash\star:\text{1}$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Delta;\Gamma\vdash s:\text{1}\quad\Delta;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=106.10385pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{m-e$\text{1}$}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 9.30548pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Delta;\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}:A$}}}}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 1:
Typing rules for
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
.

Discussion.

Rules are presented in introduction/elimination pairs, as is standard in natural deduction. In particular, bottom has no introduction rules. Our presentation satisfies Local Intrinsic Harmony
[
15
,
17
]
: local soundness (elimination rules are not “too strong”) and local completenes (elimination rules are not “too weak”). If
\lambda a.\,t
is shorthand for
{\invamp}(a,b).(t\lightning b)
, then
it is possible to add the rule in which
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash\lambda a.\,t:A^{\perp}
is inferred from
\Delta;\Gamma,a:A\vdash t:{\perp}

and drop all of
m-e
\otimes
,
m-e
\invamp_{1}
,
m-e
\invamp_{2}
,
m-e
{!}{}
,
m-e
{?}
and
m-e
1
(which become derivable), in the style of
[
7
]
for classical propositional logic. However, the reduction semantics introduces critical pairs that lead to failure of confluence
3
3

3

E.g.
, if
u:{\perp}\invamp{\perp}
and
v:{\perp}\invamp\text{1}
, then
(\lambda a.\,u\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,a)\lightning(\lambda b.\,v{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}b)
reduces to both
u\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,(\lambda b.\,v{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}b)
and
v{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}(\lambda a.\,u\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,a)
.
, so we abstain from doing so.

There are many alternative ways to formulate natural deduction for
MELL
.
Although these alternatives may be logically equivalent
(
i.e.
they prove all and only valid
MELL
sequents), they exhibit different
computational behaviors.
The rules in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
have been carefully chosen to be able to define a
well-behaved contra-substitution operation.

Lemma 9

(name=Soundness and Completeness of
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}

[Proof on pg.
B
]
, restate=[name=Logical soundness/completeness of
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
]SoundnessAndCompletenessOfCalcMELL)
.

•

Soundness.

If
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash t:A
holds in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
, then
\vdash{{?}\Delta^{\perp},\Gamma^{\perp},A}
holds in
MELL
.

•

Completeness.

If
\vdash{\Gamma_{0}}
holds in
MELL
and
\Gamma,A
is any permutation of
\Gamma_{0}
, there exists a term
t
such that
\cdot;\Gamma^{\perp}\vdash t:A
holds in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
.

Substitution and contra-substitution.

Reduction semantics for the
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
-calculus
relies on three notions of
substitution:

linear substitution
(
t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}
),

unrestricted substitution
(
t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}u:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}
)
and
contra-substitution
(
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}
).
Linear (resp. unrestricted) substitution is just capture-avoiding substitution
of a linear (resp. unrestricted) variable by a term.
To define contra-substitution, we need to adapt the notion of
linear term

to the
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
-calculus.
A
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
term is
linear
if each of the following hold:
1) each free
linear
variable occurs exactly once;
2) there is exactly one occurrence of each bound
linear

variable in the scope of its binder;
3) subterms of the form
{!}{a.t}
have no free linear variables; and
4) in subterms of the form
{t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{a}s}
,
only
a
occurs as a free linear variable in
t
.

Definition 10

(Contra-substitution)
.

The operation
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}
is defined
over linear terms
by dropping the clauses of Def.
6
for lambda abstraction and tensor elimination and adding the ones
of Fig.
2
.

For example:

(({\invamp}(c,d).s){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,t){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}=({\invamp}(c,d).s){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\langle t,\star\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}=a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash{\invamp}(c,d).s\lightning\langle t,\star\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}={\invamp}(c,d).s\lightning\langle t,\star\rangle
.
Contra-substitution is compatible with typing:

Lemma 11

(name=Type Compatibility of Contra-substitution
[Proof on pg.
B
]
, restate=[name=Type Compatibility of Contrasubstitution]ContrasubstitutionLemma)
.

If
\Delta;\Gamma,a:A\vdash t:B
and
\Delta;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash s:B^{\perp}
hold in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
, then so does
\Delta;\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}:A^{\perp}
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle b,c\rangle:=t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&\begin{cases}t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle b,c\rangle:=t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\text{if $a\in\mathsf{fv}(t_{1}),a\notin\{b,c\}$}\\
t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash{\invamp}(b,c).(t_{1}\lightning s){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&\text{if $a\in\mathsf{fv}(t_{2})$}\end{cases}\\
({\invamp}(b,c).t){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle b,c\rangle:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\\
({!}{b.t}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&\text{(impossible, as ${!}{b.t}$ has no free linear variables)}\\
t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{u}:=t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&\begin{cases}t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{u}:=t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\text{if $a\in\mathsf{fv}(t_{1})$}\\
t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash{?}u.t_{1}\lightning s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&\text{if $a\in\mathsf{fv}(t_{2})$}\\
\end{cases}\\
({?}u.t){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{u}:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\\
({t_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{b}t_{2}}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&\begin{cases}\text{(impossible as $\mathsf{flv}(t_{1})=\{b\}$ and $a\neq b$)}&\text{if $a\in\mathsf{fv}(t_{1})$ }\\
t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash{!}{b.t_{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\text{if $a\in\mathsf{fv}(t_{2})$}\\
\end{cases}\\
(t_{1}\lightning t_{2}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&\begin{cases}t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\text{if $a\in\mathsf{fv}(t_{1})$}\\
t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\text{if $a\in\mathsf{fv}(t_{2})$}\end{cases}\\
\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&\text{(impossible, as $\star$ has no free linear variables)}\\
t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&:=&\begin{cases}t_{1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\text{if $a\in\mathsf{fv}(t_{1})$}\\
t_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash t_{1}\lightning s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&\text{if $a\in\mathsf{fv}(t_{2})$}\end{cases}\end{array}

Figure 2:
Contra-substitution for
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
: we take the clauses of Def.
6
, drop those of lambda abstraction and tensor elimination, and add the ones above.

Reduction Semantics.

Reduction in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
is defined modulo an equivalence relation on terms called
structural equivalence
. We begin by presenting pre-reduction (Def.
12
), in which structural equivalence is absent, then structural equivalence (Def.
13
) and finally reduction proper (Def.
16
).

Definition 12

(Pre-Reduction in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
)
.

Denoted
\to_{\mathsf{\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}}}^{-}
, it is given by the contextual closure of the following
reduction axioms
, where both sides are assumed to be typed with the same types in the same contexts.

\begin{array}[]{llll}({\invamp}(a,b).t)\mathsf{L}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,r&\mapsto_{\mathsf{{\beta}{\invamp}{L}}}&t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}b\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}\mathsf{L}&\text{($b\notin\mathsf{fv}(r)$)}\\
({\invamp}(a,b).t)\mathsf{L}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}r&\mapsto_{\mathsf{{\beta}{\invamp}{R}}}&t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}\mathsf{L}&\text{($a\notin\mathsf{fv}(r)$)}\\
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=\langle s,r\rangle\mathsf{L}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\mapsto_{\mathsf{{\beta}{\otimes}}}&t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\mathsf{L}&\text{($b\notin\mathsf{fv}(s)$)}\\
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{u}:=({!}{a.s})\mathsf{L}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\mapsto_{\mathsf{{\beta}{{!}{}}}}&t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}u:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\mathsf{L}\\
{t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{a}({?}u.s)\mathsf{L}}&\mapsto_{\mathsf{{\beta}{{?}}}}&s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}u:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\mathsf{L}\\
({\invamp}(a,b).t)\mathsf{L}\lightning\langle s,r\rangle\mathsf{K}&\mapsto_{\mathsf{{\invamp}{\otimes}}}&t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\mathsf{L}\mathsf{K}&\text{($b\notin\mathsf{fv}(s)$)}\\
\langle s,r\rangle\mathsf{L}\lightning({\invamp}(a,b).t)\mathsf{K}&\mapsto_{\mathsf{{\otimes}{\invamp}}}&t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\mathsf{L}\mathsf{K}&\text{($b\notin\mathsf{fv}(s)$)}\\
({!}{a.t})\mathsf{L}\lightning({?}u.r)\mathsf{K}&\mapsto_{\mathsf{{{!}{}}{{?}}}}&r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}u:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\mathsf{L}\mathsf{K}\\
({?}u.r)\mathsf{L}\lightning({!}{a.t})\mathsf{K}&\mapsto_{\mathsf{{{?}}{{!}{}}}}&r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}u:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\mathsf{L}\mathsf{K}\par\par\par\par\par\end{array}

Discussion.

The first five reduction axioms correspond to introduction/elimination pairs.
The remaining ones are akin to principal cut elimination cases, and
are required for confluence (though they are not sufficient, as discussed below).
The order in which substitutions and contra-substitutions are presented on the right-hand side of
{\mathsf{{\beta}{\invamp}{L}}}
,
\mathsf{{\beta}{\invamp}{R}}
,
\mathsf{{\beta}{\otimes}}
,
\mathsf{{\invamp}{\otimes}}
, and
\mathsf{{\otimes}{\invamp}}
is unimportant since
t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}b\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}b\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}
, given that
b\notin\mathsf{fv}(r)
and also
t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}=t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}
, since
b\notin\mathsf{fv}(s)
. Pre-reduction is not confluent even in the presence of the last four reduction axioms. Consider
t=\overline{({\invamp}(a,b).\underline{({\invamp}(c,d).s){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,p}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}r}
, where, say,
b\in\mathsf{fv}(s)
. It may be reduced in two ways:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\invamp}(a,b).s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}c:=p{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}d\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}c:=p{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}d\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\lightning\star\\
\\
s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}c:=p{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}d:=\star{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\invamp}(c,d).s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\lightning\langle p,\star\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{{\beta}{\invamp}{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{{\beta}{\invamp}{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{{\beta}{\invamp}{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{{\invamp}{\otimes}}}

The terms
s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}c:=p{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}d\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\lightning\star

and

s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=p{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}c:=p{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}d:=\star{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}

are distinct. For a term
t
of type
{\perp}
, equating

t\lightning\star
and
t
would be reasonable. We next
introduce a notion of equivalence of terms that introduces
this and other equations. As it turns out, this notion of equivalence is a strong bisimulation with respect to pre-reduction.

4.1
Structural equivalence

Definition 13

(Structural Equivalence)
.

Surface contexts
S
are any of the following expressions:

{\invamp}(a,b).\Box\quad\quad\langle\Box,t\rangle\quad\quad\langle t,\Box\rangle\quad\quad\Box\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,t\quad\quad t\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,\Box\quad\quad\Box{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}t\quad\quad t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}\Box

\Box\lightning t\quad\quad t\lightning\Box\quad\quad{?}u.\Box\quad\quad\Box{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\mathtt{p}:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\quad\quad t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\mathtt{p}:=\Box{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\quad\quad{t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{a}\Box}

Note that
{\Box{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{a}t}
, and
{!}{a.\Box}
are excluded from the set of surface contexts. A
term
r
is free for a surface context
S
if
{\textsf{S}}\bm{\langle}{r}\bm{\rangle}
does not bind free occurrences of variables of
r
.

Structural equivalence
is defined as the reflexive, symmetric, transitive and contextual closure of the following axioms. In every equation, both sides are assumed to be typed with the same types in the same contexts:

\begin{array}[]{llll}{\textsf{S}}\bm{\langle}{t}\bm{\rangle}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\mathtt{p}:=r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\equiv_{\mathsf{Prop}}&{\textsf{S}}\bm{\langle}{t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\mathtt{p}:=r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}}\bm{\rangle}&(\mathsf{fv}(\mathtt{p})\cap\mathsf{fv}(\textsf{S})=\varnothing\mbox{ and }r\mbox{ is free for }\textsf{S})\\
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\equiv_{\beta\mathsf{\star}}&t\\
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\equiv_{\mathsf{\mathsf{Symm}\star}}&s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\\
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}\lightning r&\equiv_{\mathsf{split}}&t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\\
\end{array}

Discussion.

The first equation allows positive
eliminators to be propagated freely. Though note that
\mathsf{Prop}

does not propagate eliminators under the of-course constructor

{!}{a.t}
or into the left argument of a why-not eliminator
{t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{a}s}

(which are the non-linear positions). The second equation corresponds the introduction/elimination roundabout on
1
; it is not well-behaved as a reduction rule and thus is modeled as an equation. The third equation allows terms
t
and
s
both of which have type
1
to be commuted freely in their placement in an eliminator for this type. In fact,
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\equiv t\lightning s
holds whenever
t:{\perp}
.
The fourth equation states that any term of type
{\perp}
may be
understood as stemming from a contradiction. It is crucial in relating
contra-substitution and substitution. In particular, if
t:{\perp}
,
then
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}\equiv t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}
.
We emphasize that these equations apply to typed terms. For example,
\mathsf{\mathsf{Symm}\star}
should be read as: if
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash s:\text{1}
and

\Delta;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t:\text{1}
, then
\Delta;\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\equiv s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}:\text{1}
.

Structural equivalence is consistent as a theory due to the fact that
t\equiv s
implies
\mathsf{fv}(t)=\mathsf{fv}(s)
and hence
a\equiv b
is not derivable, for arbitrary linear variables
a
and
b
such that
a\neq b
. Also, all our notions of substitution are compatible with structural equivalence. In particular, if
s\equiv r
then both
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}\equiv t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}
and

s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}\equiv r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}
.
The proof of this fact relies on a series of auxiliary results involving all three notions of substitution and their interaction with
\equiv
. We highlight two of these which we find worthy of mention since they relate linear substitution and contra-substitution.
Let
\{t,s,r\}
be a set of linear terms
such that
a\in\mathsf{fv}(t)

and let
b\neq a

be such that
b\in\mathsf{fv}(t)\cup\mathsf{fv}(s)
.
Then:

1.

If
b\in\mathsf{fv}(t)
, then

t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}b\backslash\!\!\backslash r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}\equiv t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}b\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}

2.

If
b\in\mathsf{fv}(s)
, then

t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}b\backslash\!\!\backslash r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}\equiv s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}b\backslash\!\!\backslash t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}

3.

If
b\in\mathsf{fv}(s)
, then

s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}b:=t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}\equiv t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}b\backslash\!\!\backslash r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}

The main result in this section is that structural equivalence is a
strong bisimulation
for pre-reduction.
The importance of this result is that it allows to lift the notion of
reduction on terms to equivalence classes of terms modulo
\equiv
.

Theorem 14

(name=Strong bisimulation
[Proof on pg.
C.2
]
, restate=[name=Strong bisimulation]StrongBisimulation)
.

Let
t,s
be two typed linear terms.
If
t\equiv s
and
t\to_{\mathsf{\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}}}^{-}t^{\prime}

then there is a linear term
s^{\prime}
such that

s\to_{\mathsf{\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}}}^{-}s^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}\equiv s^{\prime}
.

The proof of Thm.
14

(and other results involving this notion of equivalence) is complicated by the
presence of contra-substitution and substitution in the last equation
\mathsf{split}
.
The proof is developed around an
alternative characterization of structural equivalence where
contra-substitution and substitution are absent from its axioms
(
cf.
Def.
45
in the appendix).
The following lemma exhibits some of the most interesting derived
equations (
cf.
Lem.
46
in the appendix):

Lemma 15

(Some derived equations in
\equiv
)
.

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{r@{\,\,\,\,}l@{\,\,\,\,}ll}\star\lightning t&\equiv&t&\text{(if $t:{\perp}$)}\\
t\lightning s&\equiv&s\lightning t&\\
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\equiv&t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&\\
\langle r,t\rangle\lightning s&\equiv&r\lightning(s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}t)\\
\langle t,r\rangle\lightning s&\equiv&r\lightning(s\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,t)\\
(s\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,t)\lightning r&\equiv&t\lightning(s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}r)\\
({\invamp}(a,b).s)\lightning t&\equiv&s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\langle a,b\rangle:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{r@{\,\,\,\,}l@{\,\,\,\,}ll}({?}u.t)\lightning r&\equiv&t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{u}:=r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\\
({!}{a.t}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=p{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\lightning r&\equiv&p\lightning({t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{a}r})&\\
({!}{a.t})\lightning r&\equiv&{t{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{a}r}&\\
(s\lightning t)\lightning r&\equiv&s\lightning t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\\
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=s{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\equiv&t\lightning s&\text{(if $t:{\perp}$)}\\
\star{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}&\equiv&t&\\
t{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}&\equiv&t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=r{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}&\text{(if $t:{\perp}$)}\end{array}\end{array}

We now define reduction for the
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
-calculus.

Definition 16

(
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
-Reduction)
.

Reduction in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
is defined as pre-reduction modulo structural equivalence:
t\to_{\mathsf{\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}}}{}s
, iff there exist terms
t^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
such that
t\equiv t^{\prime}\to_{\mathsf{\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}}}^{-}s^{\prime}\equiv s
.

Reduction in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
enjoys the
subject reduction
property:

Lemma 17

(name=
[Proof on pg.
C.2
]
, restate=[name=Subject Reduction]SubjectReduction)
.

\Delta;\Gamma\vdash t:A
and
t\to_{\mathsf{\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}}}s
implies
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash s:A
.

4.2
Strong Normalization and Confluence

We prove strong normalization for
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
using the technique of reducibility candidates.

Preliminaries.

Let

{\cal T}_{A}:=\{t\ |\ \exists\Delta,\Gamma.\,\,\Delta;\Gamma\vdash t:A\}

denote the set of terms of type
A
, and let

\textrm{SN}_{A}:=\{t\in{\cal T}_{A}\ |\ t\ \text{is SN}\}

be the subset of those terms that are strongly normalizing
(with respect to
pre-reduction

\to_{\mathsf{\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}}}^{-}
).
If a term
t
is SN,
let
|t|\in\mathbb{N}
be the length of the longest reduction sequence starting from
t

(which is well-defined due to König’s Lemma, because
\to_{\mathsf{\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}}}
is finitely branching).
If
A
is a type and
X\subseteq{\cal T}_{A}
,
we write
t\vartriangleleft_{A}X

to mean that
t\in{\cal T}_{A}
implies
t\in X
.
If
X\subseteq{\cal T}_{A}
,
we define its
orthogonal
by

X^{\perp}=\{t\in{\cal T}_{A^{\perp}}\ |\ \forall s\in X.\ t\lightning s\vartriangleleft_{\bot}\textrm{SN}_{\bot}\}
.
The following (typical) properties of
(\cdot)^{\perp}
hold.
If
X,Y\subseteq{\cal T}_{A}
then: 1)
X\subseteq Y
implies
Y^{\perp}\subseteq X^{\perp}
; 2)
X\subseteq X^{\perp}{}^{\perp}
; and 3)
X^{\perp}=X^{\perp}{}^{\perp}{}^{\perp}
.
Given sets of typed terms
X\subseteq{\cal T}_{A}
and
Y\subseteq{\cal T}_{B}
we define:

\begin{array}[]{lll}X\otimes Y&:=&\{\langle t,s\rangle\in{\cal T}_{A\otimes B}\ |\ t\in X\land s\in Y\}\\
{?}X&:=&\{{?}u.t\in{\cal T}_{{?}A^{\perp}}\ |\ \forall s\in X,\ t{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}u:=s{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\vartriangleleft_{\bot}\textrm{SN}_{\bot}\}\end{array}

Reducibility candidates.

To each type
A
we associate a set of terms
\llbracket A\rrbracket\subseteq{\cal T}_{A}
called the
reducibility candidates
of the type
A
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l@{\HS}r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l@{\HS}r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l@{\HS}r@{\,\,}c@{\,\,}l}\llbracket\alpha\rrbracket&:=&\textrm{SN}_{\alpha}\hfil\quad&\llbracket\alpha^{\perp}\rrbracket&:=&\textrm{SN}_{\overline{\alpha}}\hfil\quad&\llbracket A\otimes B\rrbracket&:=&(\llbracket A\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket B\rrbracket)^{\perp}{}^{\perp}\hfil\quad&\llbracket A\invamp B\rrbracket&:=&(\llbracket A^{\perp}\rrbracket\otimes\llbracket B^{\perp}\rrbracket)^{\perp}\\
\llbracket\bot\rrbracket&:=&\textrm{SN}_{\bot}\hfil\quad&\llbracket\text{1}\rrbracket&:=&\textrm{SN}_{\text{1}}\hfil\quad&\llbracket{?}A\rrbracket&:=&({?}\llbracket A^{\perp}\rrbracket)^{\perp}{}^{\perp}\hfil\quad&\llbracket{!}{A}\rrbracket&:=&({?}\llbracket A\rrbracket)^{\perp}\end{array}

Given a linear typing environment
\Gamma
, an unrestricted typing environment
\Delta
, and a substitution
\sigma
, we say
\sigma
is
compatible
with
\Gamma,\Delta
, written
\sigma\models\Delta,\Gamma
, if:
1) for all
a:A\in\Gamma
we have that
\sigma(a)\in\llbracket A\rrbracket
; and
2) for all
u:B\in\Delta
we have that
\sigma(u)\in\llbracket B\rrbracket
.

Theorem 18

(name=Adequacy
[Proof on pg.
D
]
, restate=[name=Adequacy]Adequacy)
.

For every valid judgment
\Delta;\Gamma\vdash t:A

and every
\sigma
such that
\sigma\models\Delta,\Gamma

we have that
t^{\sigma}\vartriangleleft_{A}\llbracket A\rrbracket
.

Taking in particular the identity substitution, we
obtain
strong normalization
as a corollary.

Confluence.

Given that
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
enjoys strong normalization, it suffices to prove that it is weak Church–Rosser to obtain confluence. This can be proved through a tedious case analysis.

Proposition 19
.

\to_{\mathsf{\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}}}
is confluent.

Reduction on untyped terms is not confluent. Consider the term
\overline{(\lambda a.\,\underline{((\lambda b.\,({\langle b,c\rangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{c}a}))\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,t)}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}r}
. It is not typable since
\lambda b.\,\langle b,c\rangle
does not have type
{\perp}
. It may be reduced in two different ways, as indicated by the over and underlining. However, the reducts are not joinable.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{(\lambda a.\,\underline{((\lambda b.\,({\langle b,c\rangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{c}a}))\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,t)}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda a.\,{\langle t,c\rangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{c}a}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\blacktriangledown}\,r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda b.\,({\langle b,c\rangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\blacktriangleright}^{c}a}))\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet}\,t){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\{}}a\backslash\!\!\backslash r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{\}}}=({!}{c.(\lambda b.\,\langle b,c\rangle)}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=\langle t,r\rangle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({!}{c.\langle t,c\rangle}){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}\star:=r{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{{\beta}{\invamp}{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{{\beta}{\invamp}{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{{\beta}{\invamp}{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces/}

5
Translations of classical
\lambda
-calculi into
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}

The
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
-calculus is capable of simulating Parigot’s

\lambda\mu
and Curien and Herbelin’s
\overline{\lambda}\mu\tilde{\mu}
,
as well as Hasegawa’s
\mu\textsf{DCLL}
(in the appendix due to lack of space).
We briefly recall the first two of these calculi below,
and introduce appropriate translations to the
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
-calculus.

5.1
Simulation of Parigot’s
\lambda\mu
via the T and Q translations

We briefly recall Parigot’s
\lambda\mu
-calculus
[
25
]
,
obtained via the propositions-as-types paradigm from the term assignment of classical
propositional logic in natural deduction with multiple conclusions.
Let
x,y,\ldots
(resp.
a,b,\ldots
) range over a countable set of
variables
(resp.
continuation variables
). Types and terms of
\lambda\mu
are defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\,\,}r@{\hspace{.2cm}}c@{\hspace{.2cm}}l@{\hspace{1cm}}l@{\,\,}r@{\hspace{.2cm}}c@{\hspace{.2cm}}l}\text{(Types)}&A,B\hskip 5.69046pt&::=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&{\perp}\,|\,\alpha\,|\,A\supset B\hfil\hskip 28.45274pt&\text{(Terms)}&M,N\hskip 5.69046pt&::=\hfil\hskip 5.69046pt&x\,|\,\lambda x.\,M\,|\,M\,N\,|\,[a]M\,|\,\mu{a}.M\end{array}

Judgements
are of the form
\Gamma\vdash M:A\mid\Sigma
, where
\Gamma
is a partial function mapping variables to types, and
\Sigma
is a partial function mapping continuation
variables to types. Typing rules are given by:

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 39.67337pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=79.34674pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{p-ax}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma,x:A\vdash x:A\mid\Sigma$}}}}\end{array}\hskip 1.99997pt\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 5.41669pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma,x:A\vdash M:B\mid\Sigma$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=95.84329pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{p-lam}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.\,M:A\supset B\mid\Sigma$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 2.92078pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash M:A\mid\Sigma,a:A$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=89.8353pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{p-name}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash[a]M:{\perp}\mid\Sigma,a:A$}}}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash M:A\supset B\mid\Sigma\quad\Gamma\vdash N:A\mid\Sigma$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=146.6138pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{p-app}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 37.56929pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash M\,\,N:B\mid\Sigma$}}}}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash M:{\perp}\mid\Sigma,a:A$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=78.99385pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$\textsc{{p-mu}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 2.40392pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\mu{a}.M:A\mid\Sigma$}}}}\end{array}

We write
M{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\bm{\{}}a{\lhd}N{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\bm{\}}}
for
structural substitution
, which replaces subterms of
M
of the form
[a]O
by
[a](O\,\,N)
; and
M{\color[rgb]{0.91,0.41,0.17}\bm{\{}}b{\curvearrowleft}a{\color[rgb]{0.91,0.41,0.17}\bm{\}}}
for the renaming of all free occurrences of
b
in
M
with
a
.

T-Translation.

The T-translation, first introduced by Danos
et al.

[
14
]
, embeds classical
logic into linear logic. It operates on both formulae and sequent-calculus proofs.
Here we extend it to translate
\lambda\mu
-terms to
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
-terms,
in such a way that it simulates reduction.
Reduction in
\lambda\mu
, denoted
\to_{\mu}
, is defined as the contextual closure of the following rules:

\begin{array}[]{llll}(\lambda x.\,M)\,\,N&\mapsto_{\beta}&M{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}x:=N{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\\
(\mu{a}.M)\,\,N&\mapsto_{\mu}&\mu{a}.(M{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\bm{\{}}a{\lhd}N{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\bm{\}}})\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{llll}[a](\mu{b}.M)&\mapsto_{\rho}&M{\color[rgb]{0.91,0.41,0.17}\bm{\{}}b{\curvearrowleft}a{\color[rgb]{0.91,0.41,0.17}\bm{\}}}\\
\mu{a}.[a]M&\mapsto_{\theta}&M&(a\notin\mathsf{fv}(M))\end{array}

Let us write
{!}{t}:={!}{a.(t\lightning a)}
.
The T-translation of a formula and a
\lambda\mu
-term are given by:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{{\perp}}^{\mathsf{T}}&:=&{\perp}\\
{\alpha}^{\mathsf{T}}&:=&\alpha\\
{(A\supset B)}^{\mathsf{T}}&:=&{?}{!}{({A}^{\mathsf{T}})^{\perp}}\invamp{?}{B}^{\mathsf{T}}\\
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{x}^{\mathsf{T}}_{k}&:=&x\lightning{!}{k}\\
{(\lambda x.\,M)}^{\mathsf{T}}_{k}&:=&{\invamp}(a,b).{M}^{\mathsf{T}}_{k^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{x}:=a{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{[}}{!}{k^{\prime}}:=b{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{]}}\lightning k\\
{(M\,N)}^{\mathsf{T}}_{k}&:=&{M}^{\mathsf{T}}_{k^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}k^{\prime}:=\langle{!}{{?}k^{\prime\prime}.{N}^{\mathsf{T}}_{k^{\prime\prime}}},{!}{k}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\\
{([a]M)}^{\mathsf{T}}_{k}&:=&{M}^{\mathsf{T}}_{k^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}k^{\prime}:=a{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\lightning k\\
{(\mu{a}.M)}^{\mathsf{T}}_{k}&:=&{M}^{\mathsf{T}}_{k^{\prime}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}k^{\prime}:=\star{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\{}}a:=k{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bm{\}}}\end{array}

Lemma 20

(name=Type preservation
[Proof on pg.
F.1.1
]
, restate=[name=Type preservation]TypePreservationForTTranslation)
.

If
\Gamma\vdash M:A\mid\Sigma
holds in
\lambda\mu
, then
{?}{\Gamma}^{\mathsf{T}},({\Sigma}^{\mathsf{T}})^{\perp},k:({A}^{\mathsf{T}})^{\perp};\cdot\vdash{M}^{\mathsf{T}}_{k}:{\perp}
holds in
\lambda_{\textsf{{MELL}}}
, for
k
a fresh linear variable.

Proposition 21

(name=Simulation
[Proof on pg.
F.1.1
]
